[01:42:38] And then also, as humans, we have human waste that goes to the wastewater plant with the PFAS we have in it. and it's not treated, and it's one real nasty cycle. So I'm saying all this because I want you to walk out thinking that PFAS is only in drinking water.

[01:42:54] It's literally everywhere, and really the goal is to really get it out of our environment. Now, what does that mean? How are we getting PFAS in our systems? So for those not familiar with the lab, I want to begin by describing the laboratory I direct here in Manassas, the Virginia Tech's Occoquan Watershed Monitoring Lab.

[02:24:00] And then I want to provide a high-level overview of the Occoquan Reservoir and why we consider it a, quote-unquote, one-water system. Next, I will describe what I believe to be the defining challenge for water quality in the reservoir, that is, rapid growth of population and impervious surfaces in the watershed and its impact on water quality trends and streams training to the reservoir.

[02:24:22] And then I will end with a quick overview of several community-centered research initiatives the lab is leading, all aimed at ensuring the reservoir's long-term viability as a drinking water resource. Next slide. So let's begin with a quick overview of Virginia Tech's OWML, or Occoquan Watershed Monitoring Lab.

[02:24:45] The Occoquan Watershed Monitoring Laboratory, or OWML, is an innovative partnership between Virginia Tech's Department of Civil and Environmental Engineering, the Virginia Department of Environmental Quality, Fairfax Water, and other water supply, public water supply, water reclamation agencies, city and county governments, and private consulting firms in northern Virginia.

[02:25:05] Our mission is to develop and test cutting-edge transdisciplinary solutions to emerging water quality challenges using the Occoquan Reservoir in northern Virginia as our experimental testbed. Probably the most pertinent fact to the discussion today is that the lab has been collecting water quality data in the reservoir and its tributaries for nearly 50 years. This historical data provides a wealth of information upon which to build informed policy decisions about the watershed's future, really pertaining to many of the questions that I heard you ask after Calvin's talk. Next slide. So let's talk about why the reservoir in its watershed is called

[02:25:44] a one-water system. Next slide. The Occoquan Reservoir is a drinking water supply, as you know, for up to one million people in northern Virginia. It provides raw water for Fairfax Water's Griffith Drinking Water Treatment Plant, which is the blue dot in the lower right corner of the map there.

[02:26:01] This reservoir is one of the first and oldest one-water systems in the United States, by which mean the reservoir receives inflow from stream flow and urban runoff from its two major tributaries, that is the Occoquan River and Bull Run, shown there in the middle of the map, and treated sewage from the Upper Occoquan Service Authority, or UOSA for short, which is indicated by the blue dot in the middle of the map. Next slide. Stream flow and urban runoff enter the reservoir from the two main tributaries, the Bull Run and Occoquan River. The two sub-watersheds for these streams are shown here in dark and light brown, respectively.

[02:26:39] Next slide. The water from these two tributaries, which are shown as large yellow arrows, and smaller tributaries located around the reservoir, the smaller yellow arrows, constitute the bulk of water entering the reservoir on an annual basis.

[02:26:55] Next slide. Layered on top of this stream hydrology is a large drinking water system for nearly two million people. The drinking water comes from two major water treatment plants, which are the blue dots on this map. slide. The Corbelous drinking water treatment plant sources its raw water from the Potomac River and provides drinking water for homes and businesses in the northern and central portion of the watershed as indicated by the green area there in the map. The Griffith drinking water treatment plant sources its raw water from the Occoquan Reservoir and provides drinking water for homes and businesses to the south and east portions of the map for that orange area. Next

[02:27:34] slide. The drinking water from Corbelous flows through homes and businesses and ultimately down the drain into the sewage collection network that drains to the Upper Occoquan Service Authority, UOSA, which is shown by the red boundary there. Next slide. The sewage then flows through an underground pipe network, the sewage collection system, to the UOSA treatment plant in Centerville, which is the yellow dot, big yellow dot there. Next slide. That sewage undergoes extensive treatment, and the resulting reclaimed water is discharged to Bull Run, where it mixes with

[02:28:12] stream flow and urban runoff and ultimately flows to the reservoir. Next slide. Here is a summary of the inflows to the reservoir from various sources, including Bull Run, Occoquan River, UOSA, precipitation, and other sources including and monitored streams around the reservoir like Hoos Run and groundwater. Note that on an annual basis, the Occoquan River at 48 percent and Bull Run at 25 percent are the largest contributors of water to the reservoir, followed by other sources like groundwater at 20% and reclaimed water from UOSA at 6%. Precipitation on the reservoir surface

[02:28:50] constitutes a small fraction of the annual inflow around 1%. So most of the flow that is coming into the reservoir comes from the surrounding watersheds and to a lesser extent from UOSA's reclaimed water. Next slide. Here is a summary of the outflows from the reservoir. Fairfax Water's Griffith Water Treatment Plan accounts currently for about 12% annually of the outflow, but by far most of the outflows through the dam spillway and ultimately back to the Potomac River.

[02:29:21] Evaporation off the reservoir is relatively minor at around 1%. Next slide. Now I want to talk about what I see as the defining challenge to reservoir water quality, population growth, and imperviousness growth in the watershed. Next slide. The Ococon watershed is home to 574,000 people, which as of 2020 is a six-fold increase since the 1970s, and that number is projected to potentially reach 750,000 people by 2040. By comparison, just to kind of benchmark that, the initial recommendations

[02:29:57] when the Occoquan policy was formed back in the 70s to safeguard the reservoir's water quality called for restricting population in the watershed to 100,000 people. So we're close to almost, or we're approaching 10 times that number. Another challenge for managing water quality in the reservoir is that, as was indicated here a moment ago, the watershed encompasses six different political jurisdictions, including Prince William County, which accounts for 40% of the watershed area, Fauquier County for 36% of the area, Fairfax County, 17% of the area,

[02:30:34] Loudoun County, 5% of the area, City of Manassas, 2% of the area, and the City of Manassas Park, 0.4% of the area. So there's no one political jurisdiction who can basically wave a wand and and cause everything to happen in the watershed all at once.

[02:30:51] To give you a sense of how population has grown in the watershed, in the next couple of slides I will present how the population density in the watershed has changed over time, where each of the color polygons in this map represent what's called a transportation analysis zone, or TAS.

[02:37:49] Here sodium ions are a concern, again, as Calvin pointed out, for water quality in the reservoir, both because of potential impacts on taste and also because sodium in drinking water can serve as a silent source of sodium, dietary sodium, which can be especially problematic for people on salt-restricted diets or elderly people. [02:38:55] We excrete that ultimately, and it goes into the sewer system. While the other half is sodium-rich household products that ultimately go down the drain. And there's a real opportunity potentially for doing product switching, for example, to low-salt detergents. Next, we talk about PFAS.

[02:39:16] Specifically, I'm going to be focusing in on a particular PFAS compound, PFOA, which Calvin mentioned, which is showing up in concentrations in the finished drinking water from Griffith above the EPA's recent maximum contaminant level of four parts per trillion, or PPT.

[02:39:37] Next slide. Over the past year, under contract with Fairfax Water, the lab has been collecting samples throughout the watershed and analyzing them for PFAS by EPA Method 1633. Our monitoring approach includes both fixed sites where we sample every month and floating sites where we sample once, essentially where it's like a CSI.

[02:39:56] We're looking for potential sources in collaboration with Fairfax Water and the other jurisdictions. Here you can see in color the number of samples we've collected and analyzed for PFAS so far this year. The darker green circles correspond to fixed sites where we've sampled up to 11 times so far.

[02:40:15] The lab recently acquired the analytical equipment necessary to carry out these measurements, which are highly specialized, and the lab just hired a new faculty member, Professor Karen First, who is a leading expert in the topic area. So I think we'll have everything that we need in terms of both equipment and expertise. While these data are preliminary, what we're seeing is roughly consistent with the salinity story that I just told.

[02:40:43] That is, we're seeing higher PFOA concentrations in streams and portions of the watershed, particularly in the north, in Cub Run, where in previous cover and population densities are high. So if you want to first order the formula for how this works, population growth leads to more impervious cover, leads to higher salt concentrations in streams, and higher PFAS concentrations in streams.

[02:41:09] Next slide. Based on these data, here is a preliminary assessment of the sources of PFOA entering the Occoquan Reservoir. While both Bull Run and the Occoquan River are significant contributors at 22 and 31 percent, remember there's a lot of flow, so even very low concentrations of PFOA can result in a lot of mass. We're seeing that up to 39 percent or almost 40 percent of the PFOA mass entering the Occoquan Reservoir may be coming from UOSA's reclaimed water. Next slide. All of these data are serving

[02:41:46] as a starting point for a number of community-engaged initiatives aimed at developing equitable and cost-effective approaches for addressing water quality challenges in the Occoquan Reservoir both now and into the future. Next slide. The first one I would like to describe is funded with a planning grant from the National Science Foundation Civic Innovation Challenge Program. This is a two-stage program. The first stage, which is currently what we have at the lab, provides $75,000 for a planning grant to develop with the community a stage two pilot test for some cutting-edge technology, which in our case is an interactive water quality model for the

[02:42:25] reservoir that can be used to stimulate community discussions about the future of the system. And it helps, I think, to give you a little bit of context. So in the past, the way it would work is if there was a question about the reservoir and water quality in the reservoir, the lab or another consulting firm or some other entity would be commissioned to do a study, which for a fair amount of money and about a year later, you get an answer. You might have forgotten what the question was by the time you got the answer. The idea is that we're using cutting-edge technology to make that more or less instantaneous so you can get on a model on the website and start

[02:43:02] answering and asking, asking and answering your own questions in real time. So that's kind of the goal of this, this activity. In fact, next week we are running a set of four focus groups with different community organizations or individuals, including water utility managers and practitioners, Elected officials, in fact, you all should have received an invitation to participate, business interests, and environmental NGOs.

[02:43:27] This is all building to a proposal to the NSF, which will be due in February of 2025, for up to $1 million to pilot test the model in those four communities. We are hoping, through the focus group process, to ultimately receive a letter of support to the effect that, look, if you guys build this model for us, we'll actually use it.

[02:44:31] Along similar lines, and you saw this on one of Calvin's slides perhaps, we just received word that a proposal we submitted was selected for funding by the Water Research Foundation. This project would also utilize the interactive model of the reservoir that I was just talking about that the lab has developed, specifically with the goal of identifying equitable and cost-effective solutions to meeting those EPA MCLs from PFAS we were talking about, while minimizing costs to ratepayers across the system.

[02:44:58] The participating counties and water utilities, including Prince William Water and Prince William County, are indicated on the slide here. And here's the basic idea. We'd like to investigate interventions across the watershed, including non-point source controls, pretreatment requirements in the sewer shed, as well as treatment upgrades at UOSA and Griffith, with the overall goal of meeting the EPA MCLs while minimizing the overall cost to the community, recognizing that we receive multiple bills for a homeowner, for example, from wastewater utility for wastewater services, for drinking water services, and so on.

[02:45:36] And so really what matters is minimizing the overall cost, not necessarily just sticking the problem to a single utility like Fairfax Water. Next slide. And here are my conclusions. Population and imperviousness are non-linearly correlated throughout the watershed, and specifically imperviousness increases rapidly for population densities greater than about 60 people per square mile. As imperviousness and population density increase, stream salinity also increases, as evidenced by trends in specific inductance, sodium ions, and chloride ions,

[02:46:15] so that I didn't show you that data here. Preliminary data suggests that the forever chemical PFOA is also higher in streams that drain densely populated areas. We also find that reclaimed water is a source of both salinity and PFOA in this one water system.

[03:15:59] But you generate power, you transmit it over long distances, and then you distribute it to individual homes and businesses.
